Higher rank Nichols algebras of diagonal type with finite arithmetic root system in positive characteristic
Lijie Lei, Chen Yuan, Chen Qian, Jing Wang

Abstract
The classification of finite dimensional Nichols algebras is a key part of the lifting method by N. Andruskiewitsch and H.-J. Schneider for finite dimensional pointed Hopf algebras. In this paper, we classify all rank r ≥ 5 r\geq 5 Nichols algebras of diagonal type with finite irreducible root systems over fields of positive characteristic. Using Weyl groupoids, arithmetic root systems, and Cartan graphs, we show that such Nichols algebras correspond exactly to the generalized Dynkin diagrams listed in Table 1 . Key Words : \colon Hopf algebra, Nichols algebra, Cartan graph, arithmetic root system, Weyl groupoid



1
Introduction

The theory of Nichols algebras has its origins in the theory of Hopf algebras and plays a significant role in that field. Furthermore, it has applications in other areas of research, such as Katz–Moody Lie superalgebras and conformal field theory
[
10
,
35
,
19
,
42
,
40
,
41
]
.

Classifying all finite dimensional Hopf algebras remains a long-standing challenging open problem in Hopf algebra theory
[
9
]
. The classification of finite dimensional Nichols algebras is a core ingredient of the lifting method proposed by N. Andruskiewitsch and H.-J. Schneider for the classification of finite dimensional pointed Hopf algebras
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
]
.
Nichols algebras arise canonically in the structure analysis of finite dimensional pointed Hopf algebras via coradical filtrations. For a Hopf algebra
H
, consider its coradical filtration

H_{0}\subset H_{1}\subset\ldots

such that
H_{0}
is a Hopf subalgebra of
H
and the associated graded coalgebra

\mathrm{gr}H=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}H_{n}/H_{n-1},

where
H_{-1}=0
. Then
\mathrm{gr}H
is a graded Hopf algebra, since the coradical
H_{0}
of
H
is a Hopf
subalgebra. Consider the projection
\pi\colon\mathrm{gr}H\to H_{0}
and let
R
be the algebra of coinvariants of
\pi
. A classical result due to Radford and Majid guarantees that
R
is a braided Hopf algebra and
\mathrm{gr}H
is the bosonization (or
biproduct) of
R
and
H_{0}\colon\mathrm{gr}H\simeq R\#H_{0}
.

The lifting method proceeds in three successive steps: first analyze the braided Hopf algebra
R
, then pull structural data back to
\mathrm{gr}H
via bosonization, and finally lift information from
\mathrm{gr}H
to
H

[
5
,
7
]
.
The braided Hopf algebra
R
is generated by the vector space
V
of
H_{0}
-coinvariants of
H_{1}/H_{0}
, namely Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
which is generated by
V
. in commemoration of W. Nichols who started to study these objects as bialgebras of type one
[
5
,
38
]
. Notably, the degree-one generation conjecture put forward by N. Andruskiewitsch and H.-J. Schneider fails over fields of positive characteristic, leaving an important direction for subsequent research. There exist multiple equivalent formulations for describing Nichols algebras; see for instance
[
36
,
37
,
39
,
46
,
47
]
.

Accordingly, determining all finite dimensional Nichols algebras
\mathcal{B}(V)
becomes an indispensable prerequisite for classifying pointed Hopf algebras. This motivates the following open question originally raised by N. Andruskiewitsch.

Question 1.1
.

(N. Andruskiewitsch
[
9
]
)

Given a braiding matrix
(q_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq\theta}
whose entries are roots of
1
, when
\mathcal{B}(V)
is finite dimensional, where
V
is a vector space with basis
x_{1},\dots,x_{\theta}
and braiding
c(x_{i}\otimes x_{j})=q_{ij}(x_{j}\otimes x_{i})
? If so,
compute
\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathcal{B}(V)
, and give a “nice” presentation by generators and relations.

Over fields of characteristic zero, extensive classifications of finite dimensional and infinite dimensional Nichols algebras of Cartan type have been developed by numerous authors, with representative results presented in
[
6
,
28
,
39
]
. I. Heckenberger further accomplished the complete classification of all finite dimensional Nichols algebras of diagonal type in
[
29
,
27
,
30
,
32
]
, while the generator-relation structures of such Nichols algebras are systematically investigated in
[
14
,
13
]
.

Based on these foundational classification results, N. Andruskiewitsch and H.-J. Schneider
[
8
]
established a classification theorem for finite dimensional pointed Hopf algebras under suitable technical assumptions. In subsequent series of works
[
1
,
11
,
20
,
12
]
, the full classification of finite dimensional pointed Hopf algebras with abelian coradicals was finally completed. Motivated by these fruitful achievements in characteristic zero, the study of Nichols algebraic structures over fields of positive characteristic has emerged as a research topic of great significance, offering promising prospects for further applications.

To date, finite dimensional Nichols algebras of diagonal type of rank
2
,
3
, and
4
over fields of positive characteristic have been classified in
[
25
,
44
,
45
]
. On the one hand, these low rank classification results demonstrate that the classical framework for characteristic zero cannot be directly extended to positive characteristic, necessitating innovative concepts and tools to characterize the distinctive structural features in positive characteristic settings. On the other hand, these classifications yield vital applications to the structure theory of odd-dimensional pointed Hopf algebras. Specifically, they provide an explicit characterization for the finite dimensionality of Nichols algebras associated with prime-dimensional Yetter–Drinfel’d modules. Both research directions fundamentally depend on the aforementioned classification theory as an indispensable core tool
[
21
,
4
]
.

The classification of finite dimensional Nichols algebras relies principally on two core theoretical tools: the combinatorics of root systems and the Weyl groupoid associated to such algebras. First, I. Heckenberger
[
28
]
introduced arithmetic root systems and Weyl groupoids of Nichols algebra of diagonal type based on the ideas of V. Kharchenko on Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt basis of such algebras
[
34
]
. Subsequent work extended root systems and Weyl groupoids to broader families of Nichols algebras
[
3
,
23
,
22
]
.
To quantify the scope of this generalization, M. Cuntz and I. Heckenberger completed a full classification of all finite Weyl groupoids in
[
17
,
18
]
. In particular, the main theorem from
[
18
]
reads as follows and furnishes the foundational framework for our present investigation:

Theorem 1.2
.

There are exactly three families of connected simply connected Cartan graphs for which the real roots form a finite irreducible root system
\colon

(1)

The family of Cartan graphs of rank two parametrized by triangulations of a convex
n
-gon by non-intersecting diagonals.

(2)

For each rank
r>2
, the standard Cartan graphs of type
A_{r},B_{r},C_{r}
and
D_{r}
, and a series of
r-1
further Cartan graphs described explicitly in Theorem
3.8
.

(3)

A family consisting of 74 further “sporadic” Cartan graphs (including those of type
F_{4},E_{6},E_{7}
and
E_{8}
).

This work concerns Nichols algebras of diagonal type with finite irreducible root systems over fields of positive characteristic. Our main contribution is the following theorem, which provides a complete classification of all such Nichols algebras of rank
r\geq 5
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\Bbbk
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
r\geq 5
and
I=\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
. Let
(V,c)
be a braided vector space of diagonal type over
\Bbbk
with basis
\{x_{k}|k\in I\}
satisfying

c(x_{i}\otimes x_{j})=q_{ij}x_{j}\otimes x_{i},\quad q_{ij}\in\Bbbk^{*}.

Suppose that the braiding matrix
(q_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
is indecomposable and set
M\coloneqq(\Bbbk x_{i})_{i\in I}
. Then the Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
generated by
(V,c)
has a finite set of roots
{\bm{\Delta}}^{[M]}
if and only if
the generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}
of
V
is listed in Table
1
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
reviews the fundamental notations and preliminary results required throughout this work. One key preliminary result allows us to associate a connected finite Cartan graph of rank
r
with a tuple
M=(M_{1},\dots,M_{r})
consisting of one-dimensional Yetter–Drinfel’d modules over an abelian group. For
r=1
, the corresponding Cartan graph carries no valid structural information of
\mathcal{B}(V)
. We therefore restrict our consideration to the case
r\geq 2
. A finite Cartan graph is said to be non-sporadic if it is not sporadic.
Section
3
provides an explicit characterization of finite Cartan graphs of rank
r\geq 5
. We divide such finite Cartan graphs into two categories
\colon
non-sporadic and sporadic ones. For non-sporadic cases, we first recall the construction of root systems for classical finite Cartan graphs of types
A_{r}
,
B_{r}
,
C_{r}
and
D_{r}
, as well as a family of additional Cartan graphs of type
D^{\prime}(r,s)
with
s\in\{1,\dots,r-1\}
. To enumerate all
D^{\prime}(r,s)
-type Cartan graphs for admissible values of
s
, we introduce the notion of a good
D^{\prime}_{r}
neighborhood and employ GAP software to implement its construction and computation.
We prove in Theorem
3.11
that every non-sporadic finite Cartan graph of rank
r\geq 5
is either standard or contains a point admitting a good
D^{\prime}_{r}
neighborhood. In the second subsection we further introduce the concept of good
A_{r}
neighborhoods to explicitly characterize sporadic finite Cartan graphs. The relevant structural properties are formally summarized in Theorem
3.15
. Finally, Section
4
establishes our main classification result in Theorem
4.1
. As a direct consequence,
all finite dimensional rank
r\geq 5
Nichols algebras of diagonal type over fields of positive characteristic are completely classified,
as recorded in Corollary
4.2
. In addition, Table
1
consists of all associated generalized Dynkin diagrams and in Table
2
we present the corresponding exchange graphs for the sporadic case of
\mathcal{C}(M)
.

Throughout this paper,
\Bbbk
denotes a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
\Bbbk^{*}=\Bbbk\setminus\{0\}
and
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\bigcup\{0\}
. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define
G^{\prime}_{n}=\{q\in\Bbbk^{*}|\,\,q^{n}=1,q^{k}\not=1~\text{for all}~1\leq k<n\}
.
Let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
r\geq 2
, and let
I=\{1,2,3,\ldots,r\}
. We denote by
E=(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I}
the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{I}
, and let
\chi
be a bicharacter on
\mathbb{Z}^{I}
. Set
q_{ij}\coloneqq\chi(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})
.

2
Preliminaries

We recall the standard definitions from
[
15
,
25
,
26
,
44
,
45
,
32
]
. Further details can be found in
[
24
]
.

2.1
Yetter–Drinfel’d modules and braided vector space

In this section, we recall the basic definitions of braided vector spaces, braiding matrices, and Yetter–Drinfeld modules, with particular emphasis on those of diagonal type, and establish their equivalence relation.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
V
be an
r
-dimensional vector space over
\Bbbk
. A pair
(V,c)
is called a braided vector space, if

c\in\mathrm{Aut}(V\otimes V)
satisfies the braid equation
\colon

(c\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathrm{id}\otimes c)(c\otimes\mathrm{id})=(\mathrm{id}\otimes c)(c\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathrm{id}\otimes c).

A braided vector space
(V,c)
is said to be of diagonal type
if
V
admits a basis
\{x_{i}|i\in I\}
such that

c(x_{i}\otimes x_{j})=q_{ij}x_{j}\otimes x_{i}\quad\textit{for some}\quad q_{ij}\in\Bbbk^{*}.

for all
i,j\in I
.

Definition 2.2
.

The matrix
(q_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
is called the braiding matrix of
V
. The braiding matrix
(q_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
is indecomposable if for any
i\not=j
there exists a sequence
i_{1}=i,i_{2},\dots,i_{t}=j
of elements of
I
such that
q_{i_{s}i_{s+1}}q_{i_{s+1}i_{s}}\not=1
for all
1\leq s\leq t-1
.

This paper studies braided vector spaces of diagonal type with indecomposable braiding matrices.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra. A Yetter–Drinfel’d module
V
over
H
is a left
H
-module with action
\lambda_{L}\colon H\otimes V\longrightarrow V
and a left
H
-comodule with coaction
\delta_{L}\colon V\longrightarrow H\otimes V
satisfying the compatibility condition

\delta_{L}(h.v)=h_{(1)}v_{(-1)}\kappa(h_{(3)})\otimes h_{(2)}.v_{(0)},h\in H,v\in V.

A Yetter–Drinfel’d module
V
is of diagonal type if
H=\Bbbk G
for some abelian group
G
and
V
decomposes as a direct sum of one-dimensional Yetter–Drinfel’d modules over the group algebra
\Bbbk G
.

We write
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
for the category of Yetter–Drinfel’d modules over
H
, whose morphisms preserve both the action and coaction of
H
.
The category
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
is braided with braiding

c_{V,W}\colon V\otimes W\longrightarrow W\otimes V,\quad v\otimes w\longrightarrow v_{(-1)}.w\otimes v_{(0)}

(1)

for all
V,W\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
,
v\in V
, and
w\in W
. In fact, the category
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
is a braided monoidal category, whose monoidal structure is given by the tensor product over
\Bbbk
. In particular, every Yetter–Drinfel’d module
V\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
over
H
admits a braiding
c_{V,V}
, making
(V,c_{V,V})
a braided vector space. Conversely, a braided vector space can be realized as a Yetter–Drinfel’d module over some Hopf algebras if and only if the braiding is rigid
[
43
, Section 2]
. Note that different Hopf algebras may yield isomorphic braidings on
V

via distinct Yetter–Drinfeld module structures,
and not every braiding on
V
is of the form (
1
).

Now let
H=\Bbbk G
. We write
{}^{G}_{G}\mathcal{YD}
for the category of Yetter–Drinfel’d module over
\Bbbk G
, and we say that an object
V\in{}^{G}_{G}\mathcal{YD}
is a Yetter–Drinfel’d module over
G
. Notice that if
V
is of diagonal type, then
(V,c_{V,V})
is a braided vector space of diagonal type. Conversely, any braided vector space of diagonal type can be regarded as a Yetter–Drinfel’d module of diagonal type. Indeed, let
(V,c)
be a braided vector space of diagonal type with an indecomposable braiding matrix
(q_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
of a basis
\{x_{i}|i\in I\}
. Let
G_{0}
be the free abelian group generated by elements
\{g_{i}|i\in I\}
. We define the left coaction and left action as follows

\delta_{L}\colon V\longrightarrow\Bbbk G_{0}\otimes V,\quad x_{i}\longrightarrow g_{i}\otimes x_{i},\quad g_{i}.x_{j}=q_{ij}x_{j}\in V.

Then
V=\oplus_{i\in I}\Bbbk x_{i}
and each
\Bbbk x_{i}
is one-dimensional Yetter–Drinfel’d over
G_{0}
. Consequently,
V
is a Yetter-Drinfel’d module of diagonal type over
G_{0}
.

2.2
Nichols Algebras of Diagonal Type

In this section, we recall the definition of the Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
generated by
(V,c)
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra. A braided Hopf algebra in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
is a
6
-tuple
\mathcal{B}=(\mathcal{B},\mu,1,\delta,\epsilon,\mathcal{S})
, where
(B,\mu,1)
is an algebra in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
and a coalgebra
(B,\delta,\epsilon)
in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
, and
\mathcal{S}\colon B\rightarrow B
is a morphism in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
such that
\delta
,
\epsilon
and
\mathcal{S}
satisfy
\mathcal{S}(b^{(1)})b^{(2)}=b^{(1)}\mathcal{S}(b^{(2)})=\epsilon(b)1
, where we define
\delta(b)=b^{(1)}\otimes b^{(2)}
as the coproduct of
\mathcal{B}
to avoid the confusion.

Let
(V,c)
be an
r
-dimensional braided vector space of diagonal type.
The Nichols algebras generated by
(V,c)
is defined as follows.

Definition 2.5
.

The Nichols algebra generated by
V\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
is defined as the quotient

\mathcal{B}(V)=T(V)/\mathcal{I}(V)=(\oplus_{n=0}^{\infty}V^{\otimes n})/\mathcal{I}(V)

where
\mathcal{I}(V)
is the unique maximal coideal of
T(V)
contained in
\oplus_{n\geq 2}V^{\otimes n}
.
A Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
is said to be of diagonal type if
V
is a Yetter–Drinfel’d module of diagonal type.
The dimension of
V
is called the rank of Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
.

Note that if
B\in{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
and
B
is an algebra in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
, then
B\otimes B
also admits an algebra structure in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
, whose multiplication is given by

(a\otimes b)(c\otimes d)=a\bigl(b_{(-1)}.c\bigr)\otimes b_{(0)}d,

(2)

for all
a,b,c,d\in B
, where
.
stands for the left action of
H
on
B
.

The
tensor algebra

T(V)
carries a natural structure of a Yetter–Drinfel’d module and admits an algebra structure in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
. Moreover,
T(V)
is an
\mathbb{N}_{0}
-graded braided Hopf algebra in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
. Its comultiplication
\delta(v)
and counit
\epsilon
are defined on generators by
\delta(v)=1\otimes v+v\otimes 1
and
\epsilon(v)=0
for all
v\in V
, where
\delta
and
\epsilon
are algebra morphisms. The antipode of
T(V)
is well-defined
[
7
, Section 2.1]
. Note that the multiplication on
T(V)
given by Equation (
2
) is the unique algebra structure compatible with the condition
\delta(v)=1\otimes v+v\otimes 1\in T(V)\otimes T(V)
for all
v\in V
. The comultiplication can be uniquely extended from
V
to
T(V)
. For instance, for any
v,w\in V
(we omit the tensor product symbol for elements of
T(V)
for simplicity), one computes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta(vw)=&\delta(v)\delta(w)\\
=&(1\otimes v+v\otimes 1)(1\otimes w+w\otimes 1)\\
=&1\otimes vw+v_{(-1)}.w\otimes v_{(0)}+v\otimes w+vw\otimes 1.\end{split}

Let
(I_{i})_{i\in I}
denote the family of all coideals of
T(V)
contained in
\oplus_{n\geq 2}V^{\otimes n}
, which satisfy

\delta(I_{i})\subset I_{i}\otimes T(V)+T(V)\otimes I_{i}.

Then
\mathcal{I}(V)\coloneqq\sum_{i\in I}I_{i}
is again a coideal. Consequently, the quotient
\mathcal{B}(V)
is a braided Hopf algebra in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
. By
[
2
, Proposition 3.2.12]
, the Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
is the unique
\mathbb{N}_{0}
-graded braided Hopf algebra generated by
V
in
{}^{H}_{H}\mathcal{YD}
with graded components satisfying
\mathcal{B}(V)(0)=\Bbbk
,
\mathcal{B}(V)(1)=V
, and
P(\mathcal{B}(V))=V
. Here
P(\mathcal{B}(V))
stands for the space of primitive elements of
\mathcal{B}(V)
.

2.3
Cartan graphs, root systems, and Weyl groupoids

Definition 2.6
.

A generalized Cartan matrix is a matrix
A=(a_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
with integer entries such that

(1)

a_{ii}=2
and
a_{jk}\leq 0
for any
i,j,k\in I
with

j\not=k
,

(2)

if
i,j\in I
and
a_{ij}=0
, then
a_{ji}=0
.

A generalized Cartan matrix
A\in\mathbb{Z}^{I\times I}
is
decomposable
if there exists a nonempty proper subset
I_{1}\subset I
such that
a_{ij}=0
for any
i\in I_{1}
and
j\in I\setminus I_{1}
. And
A
is called
indecomposable
if it is not decomposable.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a non-empty set and
A^{X}=(a_{ij}^{X})_{i,j\in I}
be a generalized Cartan matrix for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
.
For any
i\in I
let
r_{i}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
,
X\mapsto r(i,X)
, where
r\colon I\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
is a map.
The quadruple

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(I,\mathcal{X},r,(A^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})

is called a semi-Cartan graph if

r_{i}^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{X}}
for all
i\in I
, and
a^{X}_{ij}=a^{r_{i}(X)}_{ij}
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
and
i,j\in I
.
We say that a semi-Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}
is indecomposable
if
A^{X}
is indecomposable for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
.

The elements of the set
\{r_{i}(X),i\in I\}
are called the
neighbors
of
X
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
.
The cardinality of
I
is called the
rank
of
\mathcal{C}
and the elements of
\mathcal{X}
are called the
points
of
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 2.8
.

The exchange graph of
\mathcal{C}(I,\mathcal{X},r,(A^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})
is a labeled non-oriented graph with vertices corresponding to the elements of
\mathcal{X}
, and edges marked by elements

i\in I
, where two vertices
X,Y
are connected by an edge
i
if and only if
X\not=Y
and
r_{i}(X)=Y
(and
r_{i}(Y)=X
).

Note that the exchange graph of
\mathcal{C}
may have multiple edges with different labels. For simplicity, we display only one edge with several labels instead of several edges.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a semi-Cartan graph.
\mathcal{C}
is said to be connected if there is no proper non-empty subset
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
such that
r_{i}(Y)\in\mathcal{Y}
for all
i\in I
,
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(I,\mathcal{X},r,(A^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})
be a connected semi-Cartan graph. For any
X\in\mathcal{X}
and any
i\in I
let

s_{i}^{X}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}^{I}),~~s_{i}^{X}\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j}-a_{ij}^{X}\alpha_{i},

(3)

for all
j\in I
.
Let
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X},I)
be the category such that Ob
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X},I)=\mathcal{X}
and morphisms
\mathrm{Hom}(X,Y)=\{(Y,f,X)|f\in\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{Z}^{I})\}
for
X,Y\in\mathcal{X}
,
where the composition of morphisms is defined by
(Z,g,Y)\circ(Y,f,X)=(Z,gf,X)
for all
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{X}
,
f,g\in\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{Z}^{I})
.
Let
\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C})
be the smallest subcategory of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{X},I)
which contains all morphisms
(r_{i}(X),s_{i}^{X},X)
with
i\in I
,
X\in\mathcal{X}
. For the sake of simplicity, we write
s_{i}^{X}
instead of
(r_{i}(X),s_{i}^{X},X)
if no confusion is possible. Notice that
\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C})
is a groupoid since all generators are invertible.

Definition 2.10
.

For all
X\in\mathcal{X}
, the set

\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}=\{\omega\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}|\omega\in\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C}),X)\}

(4)

is called the set of real roots of
\mathcal{C}
at
X
. The real roots
\alpha_{i}
,
i\in I
, are called simple. The elements of
\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}=\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{I}
and
\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{-}}=\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}\cap-\mathbb{N}_{0}^{I}
are called positive roots and negative roots, respectively. If the set
\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}
is finite for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
, then we say that
\mathcal{C}
is finite.

Definition 2.11
.

The semi-Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}
is called a Cartan graph if the following hold
\colon

(1)

For all
X\in\mathcal{X}
, the set
\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}
consists of positive and negative roots.

(2)

If
m_{ij}^{X}\coloneqq|\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}\cap(\mathbb{N}_{0}\alpha_{i}+\mathbb{N}_{0}\alpha_{j})|
is finite, then
(r_{i}r_{j})^{m_{ij}^{X}}(X)=X
, where
i,j\in I
and
X\in\mathcal{X}
.

In this case,
\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C})
is called the Weyl groupoid of
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 2.12
.

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{C},(\bm{\Delta}^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})
is a root system of type
\mathcal{C}
if for all
X\in\mathcal{X}
, the sets
\bm{\Delta}^{X}
are the subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{I}
such that

(1)

\bm{\Delta}^{X}=(\bm{\Delta}^{X}\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{I})\cup-(\bm{\Delta}^{X}\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{I})
.

(2)

\bm{\Delta}^{X}\cap\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}=\{\alpha_{i},-\alpha_{i}\}
for all
i\in I
.

(3)

s_{i}^{X}(\bm{\Delta}^{X})=\bm{\Delta}^{r_{i}(X)}
for all
i\in I
.

(4)

(r_{i}r_{j})^{m_{ij}^{X}}(X)=X
for all
i\not=j\in I
where
m_{ij}^{X}=|\bm{\Delta}^{X}\cap(\mathbb{N}_{0}\alpha_{i}+\mathbb{N}_{0}\alpha_{j})|
is finite.

For all
X\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{R}
is finite if
\bm{\Delta}^{X}
is finite.

We call
\mathcal{W}(R)\coloneqq\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C})
is the groupoid of
\mathcal{R}
. As in
[
16
, Definition 4.3]
we say that
\mathcal{R}
is
reducible
if there exist non-empty disjoint subsets of
I^{\prime},I^{\prime\prime}\subset I
such that
I=I^{\prime}\cup I^{\prime\prime}
and
a_{ij}=0
for all
i\in I^{\prime}
,
j\in I^{\prime\prime}
and

\bm{\Delta}^{X}=\Big(\bm{\Delta}^{X}\cap\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}\Big)\cup\Big(\bm{\Delta}^{X}\cap\sum_{j\in I^{\prime\prime}}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{j}\Big)\qquad\text{for all}\quad X\in\mathcal{X}.

In this case, we write
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}|_{I_{1}}\oplus\mathcal{R}|_{I_{2}}
. Conversely, we say that
\mathcal{R}
is irreducible if it is not reducible.

Let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{C},(\bm{\Delta}^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})
be a root system of type
\mathcal{C}
. Next we recall the following properties of
\mathcal{R}
from
[
16
]
and
[
17
]
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{X}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
i,j\in I
such that
i\not=j
. Then
\alpha_{j}+k\alpha_{i}\in\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}
if and only if
0\leq k\leq-a_{ij}^{X}
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a connected semi-Cartan graph and
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{C},(\bm{\Delta}^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})
be a root system of type
\mathcal{C}
. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

\mathcal{R}
is finite.

(2)

\bm{\Delta}^{X}
is finite for some
X\in\mathcal{X}
.

(3)

\mathcal{C}
is finite.

(4)

\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{R})
is finite.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a connected semi-Cartan graph and
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{C},(\bm{\Delta}^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})
be a root system of type
\mathcal{C}
. Then

(1)

there exists
X\in\mathcal{X}
such that
A^{X}
is indecomposable if and only if the semi-Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}
is indecomposable.

(2)

if
\mathcal{R}
is finite then the semi-Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}
is indecomposable if and only if the root system
\mathcal{R}
is irreducible.

The following proposition implies that if
\mathcal{R}
is a finite root system of type
\mathcal{C}
then all roots are real and
\mathcal{R}
is uniquely determined by
\mathcal{C}
.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{C},(\bm{\Delta}^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})
be a root system of type
\mathcal{C}
. Let
X\in\mathcal{X}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and
i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in I
such that

\omega=\mathrm{id}_{X}s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{m}}\in\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C}),X)

and
\ell(\omega)=m
.
Then the elements

\beta_{n}=\mathrm{id}_{X}s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{n-1}}(\alpha_{i_{n}})\in\bm{\Delta}^{X}\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{I},

are pairwise different, where
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,m\}

(
and
\beta_{1}=\alpha_{i_{1}}
)
. Here,

\ell(\omega)=\mathrm{min}\{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}|\omega=\mathrm{id}_{X}s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}\cdots s_{i_{m}},i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{m}\in I\}

is the length of
\omega\in\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C}),X)
.
In particular, if
\mathcal{R}
is finite and

\omega\in\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C}))
is the longest element,
then

\{\beta_{n}\,|\,1\leq n\leq\ell(\omega)=|\bm{\Delta}^{X}|/2\}=\bm{\Delta}^{X}\cap\mathbb{N}_{0}^{I}.

Remark 2.17
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a finite Cartan graph then
\mathcal{R}
is finite and hence

\mathcal{R}^{re}=\mathcal{R}^{re}(\mathcal{C},(\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})

is the unique root system of type
\mathcal{C}
by Proposition
2.16
, that is,
\mathcal{R}
is uniquely determined by
\mathcal{C}
. And we say that
(\mathcal{R}^{re})^{X}_{+}
is the set of positive real roots at
X\in\mathcal{X}
.

2.4
The semi-Cartan Graph of a Nichols Algebra of Diagonal Type

In this section, we attach a semi-Cartan graph to a tuple of finite dimensional Yetter–Drinfel’d modules under some finiteness conditions.
Let
G
be an abelian group and
\Bbbk G
its group algebra. Let
{}^{G}_{G}\mathcal{YD}
denote the category of Yetter–Drinfel’d modules over
\Bbbk G
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{G}
be the collection of
r
-tuples of finite dimensional irreducible objects in
{}^{G}_{G}\mathcal{YD}
.
Let
\mathcal{X}_{r}^{G}
be the set of
r
-tuples of isomorphism classes of finite dimensional irreducible objects in
{}^{G}_{G}\mathcal{YD}
. For any
(M_{1},\dots,M_{r})\in\mathcal{F}_{r}^{G}
, we write
[M]\coloneqq([M_{1}],\dots,[M_{r}])\in\mathcal{X}_{r}^{G}
to represent its corresponding isomorphism class.

Fix a tuple
M=(\Bbbk x_{1},\Bbbk x_{2},\dots,\Bbbk x_{r})\in\mathcal{F}_{r}^{G}
consisting of one-dimensional Yetter–Drinfel’d over
G
and
[M]\in\mathcal{X}_{r}^{G}
. Let
V_{M}=\oplus_{i\in I}\Bbbk x_{i}\in{}^{G}_{G}\mathcal{YD}
be a Yetter–Drinfel’d module of diagonal type over
G
, where
\{x_{i}|i\in I\}
forms a basis of
V
. Then there exist a matrix
(q_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
and a set of elements
\{g_{i}|i\in I\}\subset G
such that
\delta(x_{i})=g_{i}\otimes x_{i}
and
g_{i}.x_{j}=q_{ij}x_{j}
for all
i,j\in I
. We refer to
(q_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
as the braiding matrix of
M
. This matrix is invariant up to permutation of the index set
I
and does not rely on the choice of basis
\{x_{i}|i\in I\}
. We define the Nichols algebra of the tuple
M
as
\mathcal{B}(V_{M})=\mathcal{B}(\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\Bbbk x_{i})
, which is simply denoted by
\mathcal{B}(M)
.

For a Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(M)
with antipode
S
, the adjoint action
\mathrm{ad}
is given in
[
24
]
as the linear map

\mathrm{ad}\colon\mathcal{B}(M)\otimes\mathcal{B}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(M)

satisfying

x\otimes y\rightarrow\ (adx)(y)\coloneqq x_{(1)}yS(x_{(2)})

for all
x,y\in\mathcal{B}(M)
.

In order to construct a semi-Cartan graph attached to
M
, we recall several finiteness conditions from
[
7
]
and
[
22
]
.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
i\in I
.
M
is
i
-finite if for any
j\in I\setminus\{i\}
,
(\mathrm{ad}_{c}x_{i})^{m}(x_{j})=0
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 2.19
.

For any
i,j\in I
with
i\not=j
,
the following are equivalent.

(1)

(m+1)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}^{m}q_{ij}q_{ji}-1)=0
and
(k+1)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}^{k}q_{ij}q_{ji}-1)\not=0
for all
0\leq k<m
.

(2)

(ad_{c}x_{i})^{m+1}(x_{j})=0
and
(ad_{c}x_{i})^{m}(x_{j})\not=0
in
\mathcal{B}(V)
.

Here
(n)_{q}\coloneqq 1+q+\cdots+q^{n-1}
,
which is
0
if and only if
q^{n}=1
for
q\not=1
or
p|n
for
q=1
. Notice that
(1)_{q}\not=0
for any
q\in\mathbb{N}
.

The lemma below follows directly from Lemma
2.19
.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
i\in I
.
Then
M=(\Bbbk x_{j})_{j\in I}
is
i
-finite if and only if for any
j\in I\setminus\{i\}
there is a non-negative integer
m
satisfying
(m+1)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}^{m}q_{ij}q_{ji}-1)=0
.

Let
i\in I
. Assume that
M
is
i
-finite. Let
(a_{ij}^{M})_{j\in I}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}
and
R_{i}(M)=({R_{i}(M)}_{j})_{j\in I}
, where

\displaystyle a_{ij}^{M}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2&\text{if $j=i$,}\\
-\mathrm{max}\{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,|\,(\mathrm{ad}_{c}x_{i})^{m}(x_{j})\not=0\}&\text{if $j\not=i$.}\end{cases}

{R_{i}(M)}_{i}=\Bbbk y_{i},\qquad{R_{i}(M)}_{j}=\Bbbk(\mathrm{ad}_{c}x_{i})^{-a_{ij}^{M}}(x_{j}),

(5)

where
y_{i}\in(\Bbbk x_{i})^{*}\setminus\{0\}
. If
M
is not
i
-finite, then let
R_{i}(M)=M
.
Then
R_{i}(M)
is an
r
-tuple of one-dimensional Yetter–Drinfel’d modules over
G
.

Let

\mathcal{F}_{r}^{G}(M)=\{R_{i_{1}}\cdots R_{i_{n}}(M)\in\mathcal{F}_{r}^{G}|\,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\in I\}

and

\mathcal{X}_{r}^{G}(M)=\{[R_{i_{1}}\cdots R_{i_{n}}(M)]\in\mathcal{X}_{r}^{G}|\,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\in I\}.

Definition 2.21
.

If
N
is
i
-finite for all
N\in\mathcal{F}_{r}^{G}(M)
, then
M
admits all reflections.

Notice that these reflections depend solely on the braiding matrix
(q_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
. We next recall the definition of generalized Dynkin diagram
[
31
]
.

Definition 2.22
.

Let
V
be an
r
-dimensional braided vector space of diagonal type with the braiding matrix
(q_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
. The
generalized Dynkin diagram
of
V
is a non-directed graph
\mathcal{D}
with the following properties
\colon

(1)

there is a bijective map
\phi
from
I
to the vertices of
\mathcal{D}
,

(2)

for all
i\in I
the vertex
\phi(i)
is labeled by
q_{ii}
,

(3)

for all
i,j\in I
with
i\not=j
,
the number
n_{ij}
of edges between
\phi(i)
and
\phi(j)
is either
0
or
1
. If
q_{ij}q_{ji}=1
then
n_{ij}=0
, otherwise
n_{ij}=1
and the edge is labeled by
q_{ij}q_{ji}.

The
generalized Dynkin diagram of
M
is defined as the generalized Dynkin diagram associated with the braided vector space
\oplus_{i\in I}M_{i}
.
In particular, the generalized Dynkin diagram of
M
is connected if its braiding matrix is indecomposable.

To characterize the labels of the generalized Dynkin diagram of
R_{i}(M)=(R_{i}(M)_{j})_{j\in I}
, we establish the following results.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
i\in I
.
Assume that
M
is
i
-finite and let
a_{ij}\coloneqq a_{ij}^{M}
for all
j\in I
.
Let
(q^{\prime}_{jk})_{j,k\in I}
be the braiding matrix of
R_{i}(M)
with respect to
(y_{j})_{j\in I}
.
Then

\displaystyle q_{jj}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}q_{ii}&\text{if $j=i$},\\
q_{jj}&\text{if $j\not=i$, $q_{ij}q_{ji}=q_{ii}^{a_{ij}}$},\\
q_{ii}q_{jj}{(q_{ij}q_{ji})^{-a_{ij}}}&\text{if $j\not=i$, $q_{ii}\in G^{\prime}_{1-a_{ij}}$},\\
q_{jj}{(q_{ij}q_{ji})^{-a_{ij}}}&\text{if $j\not=i$, $q_{ii}=1$},\end{cases}

\displaystyle q_{ij}^{\prime}q_{ji}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}q_{ij}q_{ji}&\text{if $j\not=i$, $q_{ij}q_{ji}=q_{ii}^{a_{ij}}$},\\
q_{ii}^{2}(q_{ij}q_{ji})^{-1}&\text{if $j\not=i$, $q_{ii}\in G^{\prime}_{1-a_{ij}}$},\\
(q_{ij}q_{ji})^{-1}&\text{if $j\not=i$, $q_{ii}=1$},\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle q_{jk}^{\prime}q_{kj}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}q_{jk}q_{kj}&\text{if $q_{ir}q_{ri}=q_{ii}^{a_{ir}}$, $r\in\{j,k\}$},\\
q_{jk}q_{kj}(q_{ik}q_{ki}q_{ii}^{-1})^{-a_{ij}}&\text{if $q_{ij}q_{ji}=q_{ii}^{a_{ij}}$, $q_{ii}\in G^{\prime}_{1-a_{ik}}$},\\
q_{jk}q_{kj}(q_{ij}q_{ji})^{-a_{ik}}(q_{ik}q_{ki})^{-a_{ij}}&\text{if $q_{ii}=1$,}\\
q_{jk}q_{kj}q_{ii}^{2}(q_{ij}q_{ji}q_{ik}q_{ki})^{-a_{ij}}&\text{if $q_{ii}\in G^{\prime}_{1-a_{ik}}$, $q_{ii}\in G^{\prime}_{1-a_{ij}}$},\end{cases}

for
j\not=k
,
|j-i|=1
,
|k-i|=1
. For
|j-i|>1
,
|k-i|>1
,
q^{\prime}_{jk}q^{\prime}_{kj}=q_{jk}q_{kj}
.
Here,
G_{n}^{\prime}
denotes the set of primitive
n
-th roots of
1

in
\Bbbk
, that is,
G^{\prime}_{n}=\{q\in\Bbbk^{*}|\,\,q^{n}=1,q^{k}\not=1~\text{for all}~1\leq k<n\}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

If
M
admits all reflections, then we are able to construct a semi-Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}(M)
of
M
by
[
44
, Proposition 1.5]
.

Theorem 2.24
.

Assume that
M
admits all reflections.
For all
X\in\mathcal{X}_{r}^{G}(M)

let

[X]_{r}=\{Y\in\mathcal{X}_{r}^{G}(M)|\,\text{Y and X have the same generalized Dynkin diagram}\}.

Let
\mathcal{Y}_{r}(M)=\{[X]_{r}|\,X\in\mathcal{X}_{r}^{G}(M)\}

and
A^{[X]_{r}}=A^{X}
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}_{r}^{G}(M)
.
Let
t\colon I\times\mathcal{Y}_{r}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{r}(M)
,

(i,[X]_{r})\mapsto[R_{i}(X)]_{r}
.
Then the tuple

\mathcal{C}(M)=\{I,\mathcal{Y}_{r}(M),t,(A^{Y})_{Y\in\mathcal{Y}_{r}(M)}\}

is a connected semi-Cartan graph. We say that
\mathcal{C}(M)
is the semi-Cartan graph attached to
M
.

Furthermore, we can attach a groupoid
\mathcal{W}(M)\coloneqq\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C}(M))
to
M
if
M
admits all reflections.

Notice that
Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(M)
is
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{r}
-graded with

\deg M_{i}=\alpha_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
Following the terminology in
[
22
]
,
we say that the
Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(M)
is
decomposable

if there exists a totally ordered index set
(L,\leq)
and a sequence

(W_{l})_{l\in L}
of finite dimensional irreducible
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{r}
-graded objects in

{}^{G}_{G}\mathcal{YD}

such that

\mathcal{B}(M)\simeq\bigotimes_{l\in L}\mathcal{B}(W_{l}).

(6)

For each decomposition (
6
),
we define the set of
positive roots

\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}_{+}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{I}
and the set of
roots

\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{I}
of
[M]
by

\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}_{+}=\{\deg(W_{l})|\,l\in L\},\quad\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}=\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}_{+}\cup-\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}_{+}.

By
[
22
, Theorem 4.5]
we obtain that
the set of roots
\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}
of
[M]
does not depend on the choice of the decomposition.

Remark 2.25
.

If
\dim M_{i}=1
for all
i\in I
,
then the Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(M)
is decomposable by a theorem of V. Kharchenko
[
34
, Theorem 2]
. The set of roots of Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(M)
is thus well-defined and denoted by
\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}
. Whenever the set of roots
\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}
is finite,
M
admits all reflections by
[
22
, Corollary 6.12]
.

If
M
admits all reflections and
\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}
is finite, then we can define a finite root system

\mathcal{R}(M)(\mathcal{C}(M),(\bm{\Delta}^{[N]})_{N\in\mathcal{F}_{r}^{G}(M)})
of type
\mathcal{C}(M)

[
45
, Theorem 1.25]
.

Theorem 2.26
.

Assume that
M
admits all reflections. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}
is finite.

(2)

\mathcal{C}(M)
is a finite Cartan graph.

(3)

\mathcal{W}(M)
is finite.

(4)

\mathcal{R}(M)\coloneqq\mathcal{R}(M)(\mathcal{C}(M),(\bm{\Delta}^{[N]})_{N\in\mathcal{F}_{r}^{G}(M)})
is finite.

In all cases,
\mathcal{R}(M)
is the unique root system of type
\mathcal{C}(M)
.

To simplify the presentation of the generalized Dynkin diagrams, we recall the notion of simple chains
[
32
]
.

Definition 2.27
.

Assume that
(\Delta,\chi,E)
is an arithmetic root system of rank
r>2
and
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,E}
is a labeled path graph. Call this graph a simple chain (of length
r
) if

(q_{11}q_{12}q_{21}-1)(q_{11}+1)=0,\quad(q_{rr}q_{r,r-1}q_{r-1,r}-1)(q_{rr}+1)=0

q_{ii}^{2}q_{i-1,i}q_{i,i-1}q_{i,i+1}q_{i+1,i}=1\quad\text{for }1<i<r

The latter equations hold if and only if

q_{ii}+1=q_{i-1,i}q_{i,i-1}q_{i,i+1}q_{i+1,i}-1=0\quad\text{or}\quad q_{ii}q_{i-1,i}q_{i,i-1}=q_{ii}q_{i,i+1}q_{i+1,i}=1.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,E}
be a simple chain and set
q\coloneqq q^{2}_{rr}q_{r-1,r}q_{r,r-1}
. By Definition
2.27
, we obtain

q=\begin{cases}q_{rr}&\text{if }q_{rr}\neq-1,\\
q_{r-1,r}q_{r,r-1}&\text{if }q_{rr}=-1.\end{cases}

Then
q^{2}_{11}q_{12}q_{21}\in\{q,q^{-1}\}
and
q_{i-1,i}q_{i-1,i}\in\{q,q^{-1}\}
for all
i\in\{2,\ldots r\}
. Moreover, we observe that the simple chain
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,E}
is uniquely determined by the value of
q
and the indices
i

(1\leq i\leq r)
satisfying
q_{i-1,i}q_{i,i-1}=q
, with
q_{01}q_{10}
defined as
1/(q^{2}_{11}q_{12}q_{21})
. In this case, we use the symbol

[formula]

to denote
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,E}
, where
q_{i-1,i}q_{i,i-1}=q
if and only if
i\in\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{j}\}
.

3
Finite Cartan graphs

In this section, we establish the key properties of finite connected indecomposable Cartan graphs of rank
r\geq 5
in Theorem
3.11
and Theorem
3.15
, which provide the foundation for our classification result in the subsequent section.

Let
M=(\Bbbk x_{1},\dots,\Bbbk x_{r})
be a tuple of one-dimensional Yetter–Drinfeld modules over
\Bbbk G
. Suppose that
M
admits all reflections, and let
\mathcal{C}(M)=\mathcal{C}(I,\mathcal{X},r,(A^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})
be the associated indecomposable semi-Cartan graph of
M
. Recall that a semi-Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}
is called standard if
A^{X}=A^{Y}
for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{X}
.

Set
\textit{A}_{r}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&-1&2&-1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&2\par\end{pmatrix}
,

\textit{B}_{r}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}2&-2&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&-1&2&-1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,

\textit{C}_{r}\coloneqq B_{r}^{\mathrm{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-2&2&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&-1&2&-1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,

\textit{D}_{r}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}2&0&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&2&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-1&-1&2&-1&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&-1&2&-1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,

and
\textit{E}_{r}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-1&2&0&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&2&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&-1&-1&2&-1&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&-1&2&-1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
.

3.1
Non-sporadic finite Cartan graphs

In this section, we present the properties of non-sporadic finite Cartan graphs of rank
r\geq 5
in Theorem
3.11
, which are crucial for our classification in Theorem
4.5
. We first recall the arithmetic root systems of non-sporadic finite Cartan graphs of rank
r\geq 5
. Motivated by the structure of these root systems, we introduce the notion of good
D^{\prime}_{r}
neighborhood characterize all such Cartan graphs.

3.1.1
The root systems

We mainly focus on the construction of root systems of type
D^{\prime}(r,s)
for
s\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
from
[
18
]
.
For the details on the construction of root systems of standard type
A_{r},B_{r},C_{r},D_{r}
, we refer to the book
[
33
, Chapter III, § 12.1]
, where Humphreys’ classical theory of Lie algebra root systems serves as a standard reference for their construction.

For
1\leq i,j\leq r
, set

\eta_{i,j}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\sum_{k=i}^{j}\alpha_{k}&i\leq j,\\
0&i>j.\end{cases}

Definition 3.1
.

Let
Z\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
. Let
\Phi_{r,Z}
denote the set of roots

\displaystyle\eta_{i,j-1},\quad 1\leq i<j\leq r,

\displaystyle\eta_{i,r-2}+\alpha_{r},\quad 1\leq i<r,

\displaystyle\eta_{i,r}+\eta_{j,r-2},\quad 1\leq i<j<r,

\displaystyle\eta_{j,r}+\eta_{j,r-2},\quad j\in Z.

Let
Y\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
. Let
\Psi_{r,Y}
denote the set of roots

\displaystyle\eta_{i,j},\quad 1\leq i\leq j\leq r,

\displaystyle\eta_{i,r}+\eta_{j,r-1},\quad 1\leq i<j<r,

\displaystyle\eta_{j,r}+\eta_{j,r-1},\quad j\in Y.

Further, let
\Psi^{\prime}_{r,Y}
denote the set obtained from
\Psi_{r,Y}
by swapping
\alpha_{r-1}
and
\alpha_{r}
.

Remark 3.2
.

The sets
\Phi_{r,\emptyset}
and
\Psi_{r,\{1,\ldots,r-1\}}
are precisely the sets of positive real roots of the Weyl groups of type
D_{r}
and
C_{r}
, respectively, as constructed in
[
33
]
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
Z_{1},Z_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
with

|Z_{1}|=|Z_{2}|=|Y_{1}|+1=|Y_{2}|+1.

Then there exists a finite Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}
with objects
a,b,c,d
such that

(R^{re})^{a}_{+}=\Phi_{r,Z_{1}},\quad(R^{re})^{b}_{+}=\Phi_{r,Z_{2}},\quad(R^{re})^{c}_{+}=\Psi_{r,Y_{1}},\quad\text{and}\quad(R^{re})^{d}_{+}=\Psi^{\prime}_{r,Y_{2}}.

Remark 3.4
.

Let
Z,Y\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
with
|Z|=|Y|+1
. Then
\Phi_{r,Z}
,
\Psi_{r,Y}
and
\Psi^{\prime}_{r,Y}
are isomorphic by Proposition
3.3
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a finite Cartan graph of rank
r\geq 3
. If there exists a subset
Z\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
such that
\Phi_{r,Z}=(R^{\mathrm{re}})^{a}_{+}
for some object
a
, then
\mathcal{C}
is said to be of type
D^{\prime}(r,|Z|)
. If there exists a subset
Y\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
such that
\Psi_{r,Y}=(R^{\mathrm{re}})^{a}_{+}
for some object
a
, then
\mathcal{C}
is said to be of type
D^{\prime}(r,|Y|+1)
.

Note that if
\mathcal{C}
is of type
D^{\prime}(r,0)
, then it is standard of type
D
, and if
\mathcal{C}
is of type
D^{\prime}(r,r)
, then it is standard of type
C
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a non-sporadic finite Cartan graph of rank
r\geq 3
. Let
a
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
whose generalized Dynkin diagram is of type
D^{\prime}_{r}
. Then
\Phi_{r,\{r-1\}}\subseteq(R^{\mathrm{re}})_{+}^{a}
.

Remark 3.7
.

[
18
, Proposition 3.8]

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a non-sporadic finite Cartan graph of rank
r\geq 3
. Then either
\mathcal{C}
is a standard Cartan graph of type
A_{r},B_{r},C_{r},D_{r}
, or it contains an object with generalized Dynkin diagram of type
D^{\prime}_{r}
.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a non-sporadic finite Cartan graph of rank
r\geq 3
, and set

\mathcal{R}_{+}\coloneqq\{(R^{\mathrm{re}})_{+}^{a}\mid a\in\mathcal{C}\}.

Then exactly one of the following holds:

(1)

The Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}
is standard (
|\mathcal{R}_{+}|=1
) of type
A_{r},B_{r},C_{r},D_{r}
.

(2)

Up to equivalence, the root sets of
\mathcal{C}
are given by

\mathcal{R}_{+}=\{\Phi_{r,Z},\Psi_{r,Y},\Psi^{\prime}_{r,Y}\mid Z,Y\subseteq\{1,\ldots,r-1\},|Z|=s,|Y|=s-1\}

for some
s\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
.

Remark 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a finite Cartan graph of rank
r\geq 3
. All its root systems can be constructed explicitly via Theorem
3.8
. In particular, we list all such root systems for ranks
3\leq r\leq 9
in Appendix
6
, where each entry records the positive real roots of a representative object. The rank
3
cases are taken from
[
17
]
and there are exactly five non-sporadic Cartan graphs, since
A_{3}=D_{3}
.

3.1.2
Good
D^{\prime}_{r}
neighborhood

Each point of a finite Cartan graph possesses its individual neighborhood structure. We compute all positive real root sets
\Delta^{[M]}
for non-sporadic finite Cartan graphs via GAP computations. Motivated by these computational results, we introduce the definition of a good
D^{\prime}_{r}
neighborhood, which enables us to cover all finite connected indecomposable Cartan graphs: every such graph necessarily contains at least one point admitting a good
D^{\prime}_{r}
neighborhood.

Definition 3.10
.

We say that
X
has a
good
D^{\prime}_{r}
neighborhood
if, after a suitable permutation of the index set
I
, there exists
a\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\textit{A}^{X}
=
\textit{A}^{r_{i}(X)}
for all
i\in\{1,4,\cdots,r\}
,

\textit{A}^{r_{2}(X)}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-1&-1&2&-1&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&-1&2&-1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,

\textit{A}^{r_{3}(X)}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-a&2&-1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&-1&2&-1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,

where
a\in\{1,2\}
and
a^{r_{3}r_{2}(X)}_{14}=0
.

The following theorem establishes a fundamental property for non-sporadic finite Cartan graphs.

Theorem 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a non-sporadic finite Cartan graph of rank
r\geq 5
. Then exactly one of the two alternatives holds:

(1)

The Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}(M)
is standard of type
A_{r},B_{r},C_{r}
, or
D_{r}
.

(2)

Up to equivalence, there exists a point
Y\in\mathcal{X}
such that
Y
has a good
D^{\prime}_{r}
neighborhood.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{C},(\bm{\Delta}^{X})_{X\in\mathcal{X}})
be a root system of
\mathcal{C}(M)
.
For any
X\in\mathcal{X}
, let
\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}
be the positive roots of
X
. By Theorem
3.8
, upon permuting
I
, there exists a point
X\in\mathcal{X}
satisfying that the set
\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}
is of the type
A_{r},B_{r},C_{r},D_{r},
or
D^{\prime}(r,s)
for
s\in\{1,\ldots,r-1\}
. There are precisely
r-1+4
distinct such possible sets of real roots. We analyze all
r+3
sets in the following way. Fix an arbitrary point
Y
and its positive roots set
\bm{\Delta}{}^{Y\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}
.
For any index
i\in I
, the reflection
s_{i}^{Y}
restricts to a bijection
\bm{\Delta}{}^{Y\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}\setminus\{\alpha_{i}\}\to\bm{\Delta}{}^{r_{i}(Y)\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}\setminus\{\alpha_{i}\}
, together with Lemma
2.13
, this determines the Cartan matrices of all neighbors of
Y
. If the Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}(M)
is standard or
Y
has a good
D^{\prime}_{r}
neighborhood, the assertion is already satisfied. Otherwise repeat the the procedure for neighboring points. Since
\mathcal{X}
is finite, the iterative process terminates. Detailed computational checks via GAP are omitted here.
∎

3.2
Sporadic finite Cartan graphs

In this section, we establish in Theorem
3.15
the structural properties of sporadic finite Cartan graphs of rank
r\geq 5
. We follow the strategy developed in Section
3.1.2
to investigate the positive real roots of sporadic finite Cartan graphs. All such roots are listed in
[
18
, Appendix B.2]
. Accordingly, we introduce the notions of good
A_{5}
neighborhood, good
A_{6}
neighborhood, and good
A_{7}
neighborhood for Cartan graphs of rank
5
,
6
, and
7
, respectively.

Definition 3.12
.

We say that
X
has a
good
A_{5}
neighborhood
if there exists a permutation of
I
and integers
a,b,c,d,e\in\mathbb{N}
such that

A^{X}=A^{r_{1}(X)}=A^{r_{2}(X)}=A_{5}
,

A^{r_{3}(X)}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&\bm{-a}&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&0\\
0&\bm{-b}&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,

A^{r_{4}(X)}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&0&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&\bm{-c}\\
0&0&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&\bm{-d}&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,
A^{r_{5}(X)}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&0&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&0\\
0&0&\bm{-e}&2&-1\\
0&0&0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,

a_{15}^{r_{2}r_{3}r_{4}(X)}=0
,

a_{45}^{r_{3}r_{2}r_{1}(X)}\not=-3
, and one of the three conditions below holds:

(A_{5_{1}})

(a,b,c,d,e)=(1,1,0,0,1)
,
(a_{41}^{r_{2}r_{3}(X)},a_{45}^{r_{2}r_{3}(X)})=(0,-1)
,

(a_{32}^{r_{4}r_{5}(X)},a_{34}^{r_{2}r_{1}(X)})\not=(-2,-2)
;

(A_{5_{2}})

(a,b,c,d,e)=(0,0,1,1,2)
,

a_{25}^{r_{3}r_{4}(X)}\in\{0,-1\}
,
(a_{45}^{r_{3}r_{2}r_{1}(X)},a_{45}^{r_{3}r_{2}(X)})\not=(-2,-2)
;

(A_{5_{3}})

(a,b,c,d,e)=(0,0,1,1,1)
,

(a_{52}^{r_{3}r_{4}(X)},a_{32}^{r_{5}r_{4}(X)})=(-1,-1)
.

Definition 3.13
.

We say that
X
has a
good
A_{6}
neighborhood
if there exists a permutation of
I
and
a\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
A^{X}=A^{r_{1}(X)}=A^{r_{2}(X)}=A^{r_{3}(X)}=A^{r_{6}(X)}=A_{6}
,

A^{r_{4}(X)}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&0&0&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&-1&0\\
0&0&-1&2&-1&0\\
0&0&-1&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&0&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,

A^{r_{5}(X)}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&0&0&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&0&0\\
0&0&-1&2&-1&\bm{-a}\\
0&0&0&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&\bm{-a}&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
,

a_{36}^{r_{5}r_{4}(X)}=0
, and
a_{25}^{r_{3}r_{4}(X)}=0
,
and either

(A_{6_{1}})

a=0
,
a_{32}^{r_{5}r_{4}(X)}=-1
,
a_{43}^{r_{5}r_{6}(X)}=-1
,

(a_{45}^{r_{3}r_{2}r_{1}(X)},a_{45}^{r_{3}r_{2}(X)})\neq(-2,-2)
;

(A_{6_{2}})

a=1
,

a_{45}^{r_{3}r_{2}(x)}=-1
,

a_{45}^{r_{3}r_{2}r_{1}(X)}=-1
.

Definition 3.14
.

We say that
X
has a
good
A_{7}
neighborhood
if there exists a permutation of
I
such that
A^{X}=A^{r_{1}(X)}=A^{r_{2}(X)}=A^{r_{4}(X)}=A^{r_{5}(X)}=A^{r_{6}(X)}=A^{r_{7}(X)}=A_{7}
,

A^{r_{3}(X)}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&0&0&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&-1&0&0&0\\
0&-1&2&-1&0&0&0\\
0&-1&-1&2&-1&0&0\\
0&0&0&-1&2&-1&0\\
0&0&0&0&-1&2&-1\\
0&0&0&0&0&-1&2\\
\end{pmatrix}
,
together with
(a_{25}^{r_{4}r_{3}(X)},a_{21}^{r_{4}r_{3}(X)})=(0,-1)
,

a_{45}^{r_{2}r_{3}(x)}=a_{34}^{r_{2}r_{1}(X)}=a_{32}^{r_{4}r_{5}(X)}=a_{32}^{r_{4}r_{5}r_{6}(x)}=a_{32}^{r_{4}r_{5}r_{6}r_{7}(X)}=-1
.

With these preparations, we establish the structural characterization for sporadic finite Cartan graphs in the next theorem.

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a sporadic finite Cartan graph of rank
r\geq 5
. Then exactly one of the following alternatives holds:

(1)

The Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}(M)
is standard of type
E_{6},E_{7},
or
E_{8}
.

(2)

Up to equivalence, there exists a point
Y\in\mathcal{X}
such that
Y
has one of the good
A_{\theta}
neighborhood, where
\theta\in\{5,6,7\}
.

Proof.

For any
X\in\mathcal{X}
, let
\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}
denote its set of positive real roots. By
[
18
, Theorem 4.1]
there exists some point
X\in\mathcal{X}
satisfying that
\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}
coincides with one of the root configurations listed in
[
18
, Appendix B.2.]
up to a permutation of
I
. In particular, there are exactly
6
,
4
,
2
, and
1
distinct root sets for rank
5
,
6
,
7
, and
8
, respectively. We analyze all these root sets case by case. For any
i\in I
, the reflection
s_{i}^{X}
bijectively maps
\bm{\Delta}{}^{X\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}\setminus\{\alpha_{i}\}
to
\bm{\Delta}{}^{r_{i}(X)\,\mathrm{re}}_{\bm{+}}\setminus\{\alpha_{i}\}
. Combining this property with Lemma
2.13
, we explicitly determine the Cartan matrices of all neighboring vertices of
X
. If
\mathcal{C}(M)
is standard or
Y
has a good
A_{\theta}
neighborhood, the desired conclusion holds. Otherwise, we recursively implement the above analysis on the neighbors of
Y
. The finiteness of the set of points
\mathcal{X}
ensures that this recursive procedure terminates in finite steps.
∎

4
The classification theorem

In this section, we classify all rank
r\geq 5
Nichols algebras of diagonal type with a finite irreducible root system.
Our main classification result is stated in Theorem
4.1
, with all corresponding generalized Dynkin diagrams summarized in Table
1
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\Bbbk
be a field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
r\geq 5
and
I=\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
. Let
(V,c)
be a braided vector space of diagonal type over
\Bbbk
with basis
\{x_{k}|k\in I\}
satisfying

c(x_{i}\otimes x_{j})=q_{ij}x_{j}\otimes x_{i},\quad q_{ij}\in\Bbbk^{*}.

Suppose that
(q_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
is indecomposable and set
M\colon=(\Bbbk x_{i})_{i\in I}
. The Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
generated by
(V,c)
has a finite set of roots
{\bm{\Delta}}^{[M]}
if and only if
the generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}
of
V
belongs to the list in Table
1
. each row of Table
1
exhausts all generalized Dynkin diagrams associated with the vertices of the Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}(M)
. For sporadic Cartan graphs, the corresponding exchange graphs are recorded in Table
2
.

By virtue of
[
25
, Corollary 6]
, Theorem
4.1
yields a complete classification of finite-dimensional diagonal-type Nichols algebras.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
(V,c)
be a braided vector space of diagonal type. The Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
is finite dimensional if and only if the generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}
of
V
appears in Table
1
and the labels of all vertices of
\mathcal{D}
are roots of unity (including
1
).

Remark 4.3
.

We fix standard labeling conventions below to eliminate ambiguities in later arguments.

(1)

Vertices of generalized Dynkin diagrams are labeled consecutively
1,\dots,r
from left to right and top to bottom. For example, the
D^{\prime}_{r}
case follows the labeling in Figure

.

(2)

Given a generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}
and indices
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}\in I
, let
\tau_{i_{1}i_{2}\cdots i_{r}}\mathcal{D}
denote the graph obtained by relabeling the vertices of
\mathcal{D}
as
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{r}
.

(3)

For a generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}

, the integer
k

A_{r}

).

(4)

For a generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}
and

, write
\mathcal{D}^{r}_{i,j}
for the diagram
\mathcal{D}
of rank
r
located in the
j
-th position of row
i
.

We verify the main theorem via preliminary analysis of several representative special cases.

Set
X=[M]
. Let
A^{X}\colon=(a_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
be the Cartan matrix of
X
,
(q_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
be the braiding matrix of
X
and
(q_{i,j}^{r_{i}(X)})_{i,j\in I}
be the braiding matrix of
r_{i}(X)
. To shorten subsequent notation, write
\widetilde{q_{ij}}\colon=q_{ij}q_{ji}
for
1\leq i,j\leq r
.

The next lemma simplifies the upcoming casework.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{D}_{\chi,E}
admits the diagram

q_{22}=-1
and

.

Proof.

If

, Lemma

yields

, contradicting Definition

. The condition

further implies

.
∎

Theorem 4.5
.

Suppose
{\bm{\Delta}}^{[M]}
is a finite set of roots of non-sporadic finite Cartan graphs of rank

. Then every admissable generalized Dynkin diagrams of
V
occurs in rows

of Table

.

Proof.

By Theorem

Case

. Then

yields
(2)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}-1)=(2)_{q_{jj}}(q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1j}}-1)=0
, for all

and

. Applying Lemma

, we separate the analysis into three subcases
\colon(a_{1}),(a_{2}),(a_{3})
.

Subcase

. Let
q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}=q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1,j}}=1
for all

and

. To shorten notation, set

. Then

.

Subcase

. Let
(2)_{q_{kk}}=0
for all integers

, where this restriction eliminates redundant symmetric configurations. Assume

q_{ii}\widetilde{q}_{i,i+1}=q_{jj}\widetilde{q}_{j-1,j}=1,\qquad\forall\,i\in\{1,2,\dots,r-1\}\setminus\{k\},\ j\in\{2,3,\dots,r\}\setminus\{k\}.

To simplify notation, set

\displaystyle\begin{picture}(0.0,3.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(2.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{10.0}}\put(13.0,1.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(13.0,2.0){\line{2}{3}{6.0}}\put(14.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{10.0}}\put(25.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(25.0,2.0){\line{-2}{3}{6.0}}\put(19.0,12.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(26.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{10.0}}\put(37.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q_{11}$}}\put(7.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q$}}\put(12.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(19.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-q$}}\put(26.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(31.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(37.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(15.0,8.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q_{-1}$}}\put(23.0,8.0){\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(22.0,11.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\end{picture}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\begin{picture}(0.0,9.0)\put(36.0,9.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(35.0,9.0){\line{-1}{0}{10.0}}\put(24.0,9.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(23.0,9.0){\line{-1}{0}{10.0}}\put(12.0,9.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(11.0,9.0){\line{-1}{1}{7.0}}\put(11.0,9.0){\line{-1}{-1}{7.0}}\put(4.0,17.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(36.0,12.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(30.0,11.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(24.0,12.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-q$}}\put(18.0,11.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-q^{-1}$}}\put(12.0,12.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(7.0,14.0){\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\scriptsize$q$}}\put(8.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\scriptsize$q^{-1}$}}\put(2.0,16.0){\makebox[0.0pt][r]{\scriptsize$q^{-1}$}}\put(2.0,1.0){\makebox[0.0pt][r]{\scriptsize$q^{\prime}_{11}$}}\end{picture}

Lemma
4.6
streamlines the subsequent arguments considerably.

Theorem 4.7
.

Suppose
{\bm{\Delta}}^{[M]}
is a finite set of roots of sporadic finite Cartan graphs of rank
5
. Then every admissable generalized Dynkin diagrams of
V
occurs in rows
11
-
15
of Table
1
.

Proof.

Define
a\colon=-a_{24}^{r_{3}(X)}
,
b\colon=-a_{42}^{r_{3}(X)}
,
c\colon=-a_{35}^{r_{4}(X)}
,
d\colon=-a_{53}^{r_{4}(X)}
, and
e\colon=-a_{43}^{r_{5}(X)}
.
By Theorem
3.15
,
A^{X}
has a good
A_{5}
neighborhood. Lemma
4.6
guarantees
q_{ii}=-1
for at least one index
i\in\{3,4\}
across all admissible configurations. Since
A^{X}=A_{5}
, the identities
(2)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}-1)=(2)_{q_{jj}}(q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1,j}}-1)=0
hold for every
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
j\in\{2,3,4,5\}
. We divide the subsequent analysis into Steps
1
–
5
according to the total count of indices
i\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
satisfying
q_{ii}=-1
.

Step
1
. Precisely one entry satisfying
q_{ii}=-1
. This case is further separated into two subcases.

Step
1.1
.
Set
q_{33}=-1
and suppose

q_{ii}\widetilde{q}_{i,i+1}=q_{jj}\widetilde{q}_{j-1,j}=1,\quad\forall\,i\in\{1,2,4\},\;j\in\{2,4,5\}.

This yields
e=1
, and Definition
3.12
forces
(a,b,c,d,e)=(1,1,0,0,1)
. Let
q:=q_{11}
,
r:=q_{44}
. The condition
a_{24}^{r_{3}(X)}=-1
gives
qr\neq 1
. Successive reflections of
X
read

the condition

Step

. Let

,

. Since

Theorem 4.9
.

Suppose
{\bm{\Delta}}^{[M]}
is a finite set of roots of sporadic finite Cartan graphs of rank
6
. Then every admissable generalized Dynkin diagrams of
V
occurs in rows
16
-
19
of Table
1
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.15
, either
C(M)
is standard of type
E_{6}
or there exists a point
X
such that
A^{X}
has a good
A_{6}
neighborhood.

Case
e
. Suppose
C(M)
is standard of
type
E_{6}
. Since
A^{X}=E_{6}
, we have
(2)_{q_{22}}(q_{22}\widetilde{q_{24}}-1)=(2)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}-1)=(2)_{q_{jj}}(q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1,j}}-1)=0
for all
i\in\{1,3,4,5\}
and
j\in\{2,4,5,6\}
. Set
q\colon=\widetilde{q_{12}}
. If
q\neq-1
, then
q^{2}\neq 1
. By Lemma
2.23
, we obtain
q_{ii}\neq-1
for all
i\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
and
q_{22}\widetilde{q_{24}}=q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}=q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1,j}}=1
for all
i\in\{1,3,4,5\}
and
j\in\{2,4,5,6\}
. Otherwise, if any vertex is -1, then
q^{2}=1
by
A^{X}=E_{6}
, which is a contradiction. Hence
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{6}_{16,1}
. If
q=-1
, then
q_{ii}=-1
for all
i\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
by
A^{X}=E_{6}
. Hence
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{6}_{16^{\prime},1}
.

Case
a
. Suppose that
X
has a good
A_{6}
neighborhood.
Then
A^{X}=A_{6}
implies
(2)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}-1)=(2)_{q_{jj}}(q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1,j}}-1)=0
, for all
i\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
and
j\in\{2,3,4,5,6\}
.
From Lemma
4.8
, we know
q_{44}=-1
in all admissible configurations. We further classify all cases into six categories (Step
1
- Step
6
) according to the number of vertices with
q_{ii}=-1
.

Step
1
. Suppose exactly one vertex is labelled
-1
. By Lemma
4.8
, we have
q_{44}=-1
, leaving only one case
\colon

q_{44}=-1
and
q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}=q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1,j}}=1
for all
i\in\{1,2,3,5\}
and
j\in\{2,3,5,6\}
. It follows that
a=0
. Set
q\colon=q_{11}
and
r\colon=q_{55}
. We get
qr\neq 1
by
a_{35}^{r_{4}(X)}=-1
.
We next analyze the reflections of

Proof.

If
q_{33}\neq-1
, then
A^{r_{3}(X)}=A_{7}
, which contradicts Definition
3.14
. We now proceed to the remaining cases.

(i)

Suppose
X
is of the type

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

where
q_{11}=-1
or
q_{11}=q
. Since
X
has a good
A_{7}
neighborhood, the transformation

\displaystyle\rule[-11.38104pt]{0.0pt}{14.2263pt}\begin{picture}(0.0,3.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(2.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(11.0,1.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(12.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(21.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(22.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(31.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(32.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(41.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(42.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(51.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(52.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(61.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q_{11}$}}\put(6.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q^{-1}$}}\put(11.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(16.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q$}}\put(21.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(26.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(31.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(36.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(41.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(46.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(51.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(56.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(61.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\end{picture}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\rule[-11.38104pt]{0.0pt}{14.2263pt}\begin{picture}(0.0,3.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(2.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(11.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(12.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(21.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(22.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(31.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(32.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(41.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(42.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(51.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(52.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{8.0}}\put(61.0,1.0){\circle{2.0}}\put(1.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q^{\prime}_{11}$}}\put(6.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q$}}\put(11.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(16.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q^{-1}$}}\put(21.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$q$}}\put(26.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(31.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(36.0,3.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\scriptsize$-1$}}\put(41.0,4.0){\makebox[0.0pt]{\sc implies
q=-1
and
p\not=2
by
a_{34}^{r_{2}(X)}=-1
, where

\displaystyle q^{\prime}_{11}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}q^{-1}&\quad\text{if $q_{11}=-1$},\\
-1&\quad\text{if $q_{11}=q$}.\end{cases}

which contradicts
q\not=-1
. Hence the cases
(a_{1})
and
(a_{2})
cannot occur.

imply that
q=1
by
a_{45}^{r_{2}r_{3}(x)}=-1
,
where

\displaystyle q^{\prime}_{11}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}q^{-1}&\quad\text{if $q_{11}=-1$}\\
-1&\quad\text{if $q_{11}=q$}\end{cases}

which is also a contradiction. Hence the cases
(b_{1})
and
(b_{2})
cannot occur.

, then the analysis splits depending on whether

q^{2}=-1
or not. If
q^{-2}=-1

imply
q=1
by
a_{21}^{r_{4}r_{3}(x)}=-1
, which is a contradiction. Hence case
(c_{1})
cannot occur. The reflection rules and constraints for
(c_{2})
–
(c_{8})
follow analogously to
(c_{1})
, so the same reasoning eliminates all remaining cases
(c_{2})
through
(c_{8})
.

∎

Theorem 4.11
.

Suppose
{\bm{\Delta}}^{[M]}
is a finite set of roots of sporadic finite Cartan graphs of rank
7
. Then every admissable generalized Dynkin diagrams of
V
occurs in rows
20
-
21
of Table
1
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.15
,
C(M)
is either standard of type
E_{7}
, or there exists a point
X
such that
A^{X}
has a good
A_{7}
neighborhood.

Case
e
. Suppose
C(M)
is standard of
type
E_{7}
. Then
A^{X}=E_{7}
yields
(2)_{q_{22}}(q_{22}\widetilde{q_{24}}-1)=(2)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}-1)=(2)_{q_{jj}}(q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1,j}}-1)=0
for all
i\in\{1,3,4,5,6\}
and
j\in\{2,4,5,6,7\}
. Set
q\colon=\widetilde{q_{12}}
.
Since
A^{X}=E_{7}
, we have
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{7}_{20,1}
when
q\neq-1
, and
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{7}_{20^{\prime},1}
when
q=-1
.

Case
a
. Suppose
X
has a good
A_{7}
neighborhood. Since
A^{X}=A_{7}
, we have
(2)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}-1)=(2)_{q_{jj}}(q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1,j}}-1)=0
for all
i\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
and
j\in\{2,3,4,5,6,7\}
. Additionally, we obtain
q_{33}=-1
for all cases by Lemma
4.10
.
We categorize all possible configurations based on the number of vertices labeled
-1
, which yields the seven cases treated in Steps
1
–
7
.

Step
1
. Suppose exactly one vertex is labelled
-1
, namely
q_{33}=-1
and
q_{ii}q_{i,i+1}^{\prime}=q_{jj}q_{j-1,j}^{\prime}=1
for all
i\in\{1,2,4,5,6\}
and
j\in\{2,4,5,6,7\}
. Set
r\colon=q_{11}
and
q\colon=q_{44}
. Then we obtain
rq\not=1
by
a_{24}^{r_{3}(X)}=-1
. The reflections of
X

,

, and

and

. When

. Hence we obtain

by
a_{32}^{r_{4}(X)}=-1
. And Lemma

(a_{2})

4.10

(a_{1})
precludes such a case.

Consider the symmetric case that

and

. Similarly to the proof above, this case is ruled out by Lemma

(a_{2})
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Suppose
\bm{\Delta}^{[M]}
is the finite root system of a sporadic finite Cartan graph of rank
8
. Then every admissible generalized Dynkin diagram of
V
appears in the
22
nd row of the table
1
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.15
,
C(M)
is standard of type
E_{8}
. Then
A^{X}=E_{8}
yields
(2)_{q_{22}}(q_{22}\widetilde{q_{24}}-1)=(2)_{q_{ii}}(q_{ii}\widetilde{q_{i,i+1}}-1)=(2)_{q_{jj}}(q_{jj}\widetilde{q_{j-1,j}}-1)=0
for all
i\in\{1,3,4,5,6,7\}
and
j\in\{2,4,5,6,7,8\}
. Set
q\colon=\widetilde{q_{12}}
. By
A^{X}=E_{8}
, one obtains
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{8}_{22,1}
if
q\neq-1
and
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{8}_{22^{\prime},1}
if
q=-1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

The forward implication is straightforward. Indeed, suppose the generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}
of
V
lies in any row
s
or
s^{\prime}
of Table
1
. By Lemmas
2.20
and
2.23
,
M
admits all reflections, so we may attach a Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}(M)
to
V
via Theorem
2.24
. This Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}(M)
coincides with the one constructed from the Dynkin diagrams in row
s
of Table 4 in
[
32
]
, as recorded in the third column of Table
2
. Furthermore, the arithmetic root systems associated to this Cartan graph are finite; see
[
32
, Theorem 22]
. Consequently, the Weyl groupoid
\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{C}(M))
is finite, and Theorem
2.26
implies that
\mathcal{B}(V)
possesses a finite set of roots
\Delta^{[M]}
.

Next we prove the converse direction. Assume that
\mathcal{B}(V)
has a finite set of roots
{\bm{\Delta}}^{[M]}
. By Theorem
2.26
,
\mathcal{C}(M)
is a finite Cartan graph. According to Theorem
1.2
, any such finite Cartan graph
\mathcal{C}(M)
is either non-sporadic or sporadic. If
\mathcal{C}(M)
is non-sporadic , Theorem
4.5
implies that the generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}
of
V
belongs to rows
1
to
10
of Table
1
. If
\mathcal{C}(M)
is sporadic, it follows from Theorem
4.7
,
4.9
,
4.11
, and
4.12
that the generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}
of
V
lies in rows
11
to
22
of Table
1
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

If a generalized Dynkin diagram
\mathcal{D}
appears in a row of Table
1
, then that row contains exactly the generalized Dynkin diagrams of all points of
\mathcal{C}(M)
. The corresponding exchange graphs for the sporadic case of
\mathcal{C}(M)
are listed in Table
2
.

Appendix

For brevity, we adopt the product notation
\prod_{i=1}^{r}i^{x_{i}}
as a shorthand for the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}\alpha_{r+1-i}
.
